section (i) of section 5 rea dwith section 6 of the Delhi

ment of Rajasthan vide order No. F.24(3) Home*Gr. S|92

the whole of the State of Rajasthan for investigation of

(i) Offences punishable under sections 147, 148, 149, 

325, 342, 379 and 307 IPC relating to case in 
Crime No. 219(92 registered in Police Station, 
Kunther, Distt. Bharatpur, Rajasthan.

427, 336 of IPC and under section 3 PDPP Act

323, 307 IPC, to case in Crime No. 222(92 regis¬ 
tered in Police Station Kumher, Distt Bharatpur, 
Rajasthan. 

(iv) Offences punishable under Sections 147, 148, 149, 

307, 395, 427 of IPC relating to case in Crime No. 
223(92 registered in Police Station Kumher, Distt. 
Bharatpur, Rajasthan. 

(v) Offences punishable under sections 147, 148, 149, 323, 
307, 336, 427 IPC and 3 SC|ST Act relating to 
case in Crime No. 224(92 registered in Police 
Station Kumher, Distt. Bharatpur, Rajasthan. 

(vi) Offences punishable under section 436 IPC and 3 

SC|ST Act relating to case in Crime 225(92 regis¬ 
tered in Police Station Kumher, Distt. Bharatpur, 
Rajasthan. 

(vii) Offences punishable under section 307 IPC, 4/5 Ex¬ 

plosive Act relating to case in Crime No. 226792 
registered in Police Station Kumher, Distt. Bharat¬ 
pur, Rajasthan. 

(yiii) Offences punishable under section 4/5 Explosive Act 
relating to case m Crime No. 227(92 registered, m

New Delhi, the 17th June, 1992 

S.O. 1762.—In exercise of the powers conferred by 
section 3 of the Delhi Special Police Establishment Act, 
1946 (Act No. 25 of 1946 ), the Central Government hereby 
specifies the following offences as the offences which are to 
be investigated by the Delhi Special Police Establishment, 
namely t— 

(a) Offences putrsbable under sections 3 and 4 of the 
Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tubes (Prevention 
of Atrocities) Act, 1989; and 

(b) Attempts, abetments and conspiracies in relation to 

or in connection with one or more of the offences 
mentioned above and any other offence or offences 
committed in the course of the same transaction. 

[No. 228/27/92-AVD. II] 

A. K. PARASHAR, Dy. Secy.

(Aichacologicyl Sujv r cy of fjuli .0 
Ncvv Delhi, the 16th June. 1092 
(ARCHALOLOGY) 

S.O. 1764.—Wheieus by the notificalicn of the Government 
of India in the Department of Culture, Archaeological Survey 
of India No. S.O. 1447 dated the 15th May. 1991, published 
in the Gazelle of India, Part 11 Sections 3 sub-section (ii) 
dated the 251h May, 1991, the Cential Government gave one 
month’s notice of its intention to declare areas upto 100 
metcis from the protected limits, and further beyond it upto 
200 meters near or adjoining protected monuments to be 
piohibited and regulated areas respectively foi purposes of 
both mining operation and constiuction; 

And whereas the said Gazette was made available to the 
public on the 5th June, 1991; 

And vvheuas objections to the making of ^lch declaration 
received from the person interested in the said areas have 
been considered by the Central Government. 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers confcned by rule 
32 of the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and 
Re mu ms Rules, 1959, the Central Government hereby dec¬ 
lares the s-aid areas to be prohibited and regulated areas. 
This shall be in addition to and not in any way prejudice the 
similar declarations already made in respect of monuments 
at Fatchpur Sikri; JMahabalipuram; Golconda Fort, Hyderabad 
(Andhda Piadesh); Thousand Pillaied Temple, Hanamkonda, 
Distiict Waiangul (Andhiu Pradesh); Sher Sbah's Tomb, 
Sasarum (Bihui): Rock Edict of Ashoka, Kopbul, District 
Raichur (Karnataka); Fort Wall, Bijapur (Karnataka); 
Gomuleswora Statue at Sravanbclgoln, District Hassan 
(Karnataka); Elephanta Caves, Ghainpur, District Kolaba 
IMahnrshtrn),

New Delhi, the 15th June, 1992 

S O, 1768.—Whereas by notification of the Government of 
India in the Ministry of Petroleum & Natural Gas S.O. No. 

2627 dated 17-9-90 under sub-section (1) of Section 3 of 
the Petroleum and Minerals Pipelines fAcquisit.on of Right 
of User in Land) AcL 1962 (50 of 1962), the Central 
Government declared it’s intention to acquire the right of 
user in lands specified in the schedule appended to that noti¬ 
fication for the purpose of laying pipeline. 

And whereas the Competent Authority has under sub¬ 
section (1) of the Secion 6 of the said Act,siibmitted report 
to the Government; 

And further whereas the Central Government has, after 
considering the said report decided to acquire the right 
user in the lands in the Schedule appended to this notification; 

Now, therefore, jn exercise of the power conferred by sub¬ 
section (1) of the Section 6 of the said Act the Central 
Government hereby declares that the right of user in the 
said laud specified in the schedule appended to this notifica¬ 
tion hereby acquired for laying the pipeline ; 

And further in exercise of power conferred by sub-section 
(4) of the section, the Central Government directs that the 
right of user in the said lands shall instead of vesting in 
Central Government vests on thifl date of the publication 
of this declaration in the Oil & Natural Gas Commission 
free from encumbrances,

fication for the purpose of laying pipeline.

New Delhi, the 5th June, 1992 

S,0. 1774,—Whereas by notification of the Government of 
India in the Ministry of Petroleum & Natural Gas S.O. 
No. 421 dated 25-1-1991 under sub-section (1) of Section 3 
of the Petroleum and Minerals Pipelines (Acquisition of 
Right of User in Land) Act, 1962 (50 of 1962), the Cen¬ 
tral Government declared it’s intention to acquire the right 
cf user in lands specified in the schedule appended to that 
notification for the purpose of laying pipeline; 

And whereas the Competent Authority has undci suh- 
scction (1) of Section 6 of the said Act, submitted to the 
Government; 

And further whereas the Central Government has, after 
considering the said report decided to acquire the right 
of user in the lands specified in the Schedule appended to 
this notification ; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the power conferred by sub¬ 
section (1) of Section 6 of the said Act, the Central 
Government hereby declares that the right of user in the 
said land specified in the schedule appended to this notifica¬ 
tion hereby acquired for laying the pipeline: 

And further in exerc'se of power conferred by sub-section 
(4) of the section, the Central Government directs that the 
right of user in the said lands shall instead of vesting in 
Central Government vests on this date of the publicaion of 
this declaration in the 01 & Natural Gas Commission free 
from encumbrances.

AWARD 

The Government of India in the Miinstry of Labour in 
exercise of the powers conferred on them by clause (c) of 
sub-section (1) and sub-section (2-A) of Section 10 of the 
Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, has referred the following 
dispute to this Tribunal for adjudication vide MinistivT 
Order No. L-22G12(162) 190-IR (C-II) dated 30-11-1990. 

SCHEDULE 

"Whether the management of Nimcha Colliery under 
Satgram Area of M/s. E.C. Ltd., was justified in 
not accepting Shri Jailal Yadav, Overman’s date 
of birth as 16-5-1949 recorded in Statutory Certi¬ 
ficate as authentic and final ? If not. to what 
relief the workman Je entitled V 


Employers hi ielation to the Management of Madhav¬ 
pur Colliery of Kajora Area of MG. E.C, Ltd. 

AND 

Their workman.

For the Workman—Sri Bijoy Kumar, Joint Secretin\ 
of the Union. 

INDUSTRY : Coal. STATE : West Bangui. 

Dated, the 29th May, 1992 
AWARD 

The Government of India in the Ministry of l abour in 
exercise of the powers conferred on them by clause (d) 
of sub-section (1) and sub-section (2-A) of Sect on 10 of

2. The case of the union in brief is that all the present 
eleven workmen of this Reference had been working: at Lower 
Kenda ColTery In underground jobs deployed by the local 
administration. They used to be deployed through con¬ 
tractors like other contract labours engaged in such under¬ 
ground operations before and after take over of the non¬ 
coking coal mines and continued to be £o deployed e\cn 
idler nationalisation in 1973. 

The present workers were connected with eu.il nioJuUion 
and their deployment was violating the prov sions or Comma 
Labour (Regulation and Aboliion) Act, 1^70. So them 
was agitation from the union as well as from PoLtical parlies 
to do away the contract system and as a rcaiill th„ lonli tu¬ 
tors’ labour weie taken on the roll of the Company in 1974. 

That all other woikers of The contractors of New Kenda 
Colliery were taken on the io]l of the orient employer, But 
the present eleven workmen were left cut for the reasons 
best known to the management. Be'ng aggrieved, the matter 
was taken up at various level and having failed fo t'N relief 
the matter wan taken tip for conciliation. The conciliation 
ended in failure and the mailer was sen! to tho Nfinis!iy of 
Labour, Government of Ind a. Ultimately the Ministry of 
Labour has referred the dispute to this Tribunal foi adjudi¬ 
cation. 

3- The management has Rled written objection contend¬ 
ing inter-alin that the present Reference is not maintainable 
Rnd this Tnbundl has no jurisdiction on the face on the 
language of the schedule of the Reference. 

The management has denied about the existence of anv 
worker named malum Bouri a$ mentioned in SI No. 2 of 
the Autiexure of the Reference. The oilier workmen of the 
Reference wei© serving in the establishment of contractors 
when, it was permissible for mine owners to engage contract 
labour^ h tbo underground work of the coal mine. That 
with the introduc ion of the piovisions of engaging contract 
labours in several works in coal mine under the Notification 
by the Central Government, under Contract labour (Regula¬ 
tion and Abolition) Act, 1970, the faith rr derknmert of 
the said persons as contract labour n the Colliery became 
impermissible. 

The public sector employer has absorbed some of the 
woikmcn previously working under the contractor* wherever 
such vacancy were available Although the employer had no 
legal obligation to absoib such workmen who worked under 
contractor?. It was not feasible to absoib the present work¬ 
men No vacancy was available to absorb fh' present work¬ 
men after the letter written by the V.ldL Chief Personnel 
Officer givng assurance to the workmen. The management 
has expressed in the said letter that the AddL Chief Person¬

when there will be vacancy.

tho roll of the company, b,t the present workmen were not

find that the main point of the union is that the manage¬ 
ment has discriminated bv not taking the present woikmcn 
In service. So in yiew of the circumstances stated above I 
find-, thkt the present case is not covered bv the principles

provisions of Contract Labour (Regulation and Abolition) 
Act. The Schedule of the Reference reads as follows ;

AWARD 

1 The Central Government, Ministry of Labour, New 
Delhi, has referred the following disputes to this Tribunal 
vide their notification nos. mentioned herein below for ad¬ 
judication— 

Vide Notification No. L*12012/284/86-DJI(A> dated nil : 

Whether the action of the management of State Bank of 
Patiala in relation to their Naveen Market Branch in 
terminating the services of Sri Keshav Kumar Shukla 
w.e.f 16-2-80 and not considering him for further 
employment while recruiting fresh hands under sec. 
25H of the I. D. Act, is justified ? If not, to what 
relief the workman concerned is entitled ? 

Vide Notification No. L-12012/286/86-D. 11(A) Dt. 31-8-87: 

Whether the action of the management of State Bank 
of Patiala, Naveen Market Branch, Kanpur in termi¬ 
nating the services of Sri B. N. Kapoor w.e.f. 
16-10-80 and not considering him for further emp¬ 
loyment while recruiting fresh hands under sec. 25H 
of the I. D. Act. is justified ? If not, to what relief 
the concerned workman is entitled ? 

Vide Notification No. Lr 12012/424/86-D. 11(A) dated 
25-8-87 : 

Whether the action of the management of State Bank of 
Patiala, Naveen Market Branch, Kanpur in termi¬ 
nating the services of Sri A. P. Awasthi, ex-cashier- 
cum-clerk w.e.f. 26-9-78 is justified ? If not, to what 
relief is the concerned workman entitled ? 

2. Smce in all the above three Industrial Dispute cases a 
common question ot fact and law is involved as such they 
were consolidated by me by means of my order dt. 12*-2-90 
and I. D. No. 112/87 was made the leading case. 

3. The Industrial Dispute Nos. 112/87 and 122/87 have 
been raised bv the workmen themselves and I. D. No. 114787 
(has been raiser! through the Secretary U. P, Bank Emp¬ 
loyees Union, Kanpur. 

4 It is the common case in all the above industrial dis¬ 
putes that the Bank with a wew to deprive the workmen 
from the benefits of modified Sastry Award/Bipartite Settle¬ 
ments and regvforisation etc. started a pract ? ce to appoint 
fern nor arv h*nds for dofog the work of regular nature. It is 
further pleaded that such temperarv employees were retren¬ 
ched and fresh band*! were appointed to avoid absorption in 
terms of the Bank’s Instructions, which was an unfair labour 
practice besides being illegal and unjustified. It is furthei 
averred that the workmen w<re not the iunior most at the 
time of their jefrenenment and that no letters of appoint¬ 
ment /letters of termination nor any notice or notice pay was 
ever yiven to the workmen at the time of termination of 
fhrir services. 

Th^ case of the Union \n respect of workman Sri A. P. 
Awasfibi U D No 114/87) U that he was appointed bv the 
BsmV ntyamst n rpmilar post of cariuer-cunvclerk at Naveen 
MarVnf Branch Batumi*, op 3-7-78 and worked unto 25-9-78 
for 8 5 d^vs. wWpmVr his services were terminated without 
anv imrificarion nr reason. 

6 The case of workman Sri B. N. Karoor (1 D. No. 
np/ST) is fha* ho was appointed bv the bank against a resn- 
lar post of clerk-cum-cashier at its Naveen Market Branch, 
1570 GI/92—11 


Kanpur, on 23-7-80, and worked upto 15-10-80 for 86 days, 
whereafter, his jervices were terminated by the bank with¬ 
out any justification and leason. 

7. Similar is the case of workman Sri Keshav Kumar Shukla 
(I. D. No. 112/87). The workman alleges that he was also 
appointed b> the bank management against a regular post of 
clerk-cum-cashier on 7-12-79 a i its Naveen Market Branch, 
Kanpur. In all he had worked for 71 days upto 16-2-80 
when his services were terminated the same day on the 
ground of absence. 

8. Besides the above pleas their common case is that the 
services of the workmen were terminated in utter disregard 
of sec. 25G and 25H of the I. D. Act, read with section 
251 & I.D. (Central) Rules, Violation of Sastry’s Award paras 
493, 495, 507, 516, 519, 522 and 524 read with paras 20.7 
and 20.8 of the 1st Bipartite Settlement and violation of Ar¬ 
ticles 14, 16 and 21 of the Constitution of India have also 
been alleged by the workmen. 

9. In all the three cases ptayei has been made for the re¬ 
instatement in service of all the three workmen with full back 
wages and all consequential benefits. 

10. The cases have been contested by the management of 
State Bank of Patiala. The management in their reply in 
respect ol the above three workmen while disputing the fact 
that they had been appointed on any regular or permanent 
post of clerk-cum-cashier have admitted the number of their 
working days as given by them in their claim statement. Man¬ 
agement plead that as per policy of the Government of India, 
recruitment of clerical staff on regular basis for the bank 
as also for any other nationalised bank can now be done 
bv the Banking Services Recruitment Board and not by in¬ 
dividual banks. The bank was and is empowered to recruit 
by itself only temporary hands in the clerical cadre for a 
specified h’nrted period only, the recruitment on a regular 
basis bemg the prerogative of the Banking Service Recruit¬ 
ment Board. Therefore, an appointment on a temporary post 
for limited specified period is bound to come to an end on 
the exniiv of the Limited period. The bank further plead 
that since the workmen had not been appointed to fill m per¬ 
manent vacancies, the question of the rariod of their tenv 
porarv employment counting towards probationary rermd does 
not arise The hank have admitted that the workman Sri A. 
P, Awasthi was appointed on 3-7-78 tT D No 114 /R* 7 ) Sri 
B N Korroor was a nn o’oted on 23-7-80 /T. D No 17^/87) 
anrf Vpoh*v Kumar sfmVia was anno?on 7-10-^9 but 

n nno!nfm°ntc wata rn^d“ for r><=>r*nr!c +0 ro^A wbfi 

the temporary increase of work. The management nlead that 
the concept of seniority juniority relates essentially to per¬ 
manent employees constituting the permanent strength of a 
particular cadre and not for employees eng^ed for specified 
periods of temporary hands . Smce the workmen had been 
appointed for 85 days (Sri A. P. Awasthi of I. D No. 114/87 
and Sri B N. Karoor of I D No. 122/87* and 71 davs (Sri 
Keshav Kumar Shukla of I D No. 112/87) respectively and 
davs in a period of 12 months they were not entitled to receive 
dav* in period of 12 months thev were not entitled to receive 
anv compensation under section 25F I. D. Act. The manage¬ 
ment dispute the application of section 250 & 25H of the 
Act to the cases of these three workmen. The management 
further dispute violation of anv provisions of the Constitu¬ 
tion Tndia or anv para of the Sastry Award or Bipartite 
Settlement. 

11. In support of their respective cases all the three work;- 
men have examined themselves and have also rehed upon 
documents filed by them. On the other hand, in support of 
their case, the management ha\e examined Sri Rakesh Vohra, 
an Officer of the Bank. 

12. From the pleadings of the parties it is abundantly clear 
that none of the workmen had completed one year’s continu¬ 
ous service preceding the date of their termination. They had 
been appointed by the bank ior fixed periods of time to cope 
with the" temporary increase of work. Workman S/Sri A. r. 
Awasthi and Sri B„ N. Kapoor were appointed for 85 days 
and workman Sri Keshav Kumar Shukla had been appointed 
for 71 days. There is no dispute about the number of word¬ 
ing days of the workmen between the parties. Therefore, 



2946 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: JULY 4, 1 992/ASADHA 13, 1914 [Part II— Sic. 3(b)] 


having worked for much less than 240 days in their case 
the provisions of section 25b, 250 & 25B of the I. D. Act 
would not apply. For the application of section 25F I. D. 
Act it is necessary that the workmen must have been in con¬ 
tinuous service for not less than one year under any employer 
prior to the date of his tet mutation /retrenchment’ SEc. 25G 
of the 1. D. Act i s to be lead with rules 77 of the (I. D. 
Central Rules) and Sec. 25H ct the I. D. Act is to be read 
with Rule 78 Rules 77 and 78 apply to a workman as defined 
in Rule 76 of the I. D. Central Rules. According to Rule 76 
such a workman should be a person who had worked con¬ 
tinuously for not less than than one year prior to the termi¬ 
nation of his services. Continuous one year service within 
the meaning of Sec 25B 1. U. Act meam working for 240 
days atleast during a \ear. Therefore, in their case the pro¬ 
visions of section 25G and 25H of the Act too are not 
applicable. 

J'* The woikmen neither in their claim statement nor in 
then rejoinder had named the persons who were junior to 
them at the time of their retrenchment nor they had given 
the names of those persons who had been engaged by the 
b’mk subsequent to their retrenchment. The management 
witness in para 22 of his affidavit has specifically deposed 
that no such appointments of temporary nature for specified 
durations were ever made after 1980. 

14 I may make it clear that it is a matter of common 
knowledge that recruitment i'a the banking industry are 
being made through Banking Service Recruitment Board since' 
1978. against regular pods. Therefore, individual banks are 
not empowered to recruit persons of their own accord. In 
view of it none of these three workmen ever got any vested 
oi legal right of claiming any appointment in the clerical 
cadie of the bank or even for claiming continuance of the 
lemporaiy appointments bevod the specific periods for which 
the} were engaged due to exigencies of work. 

15. Workman Sri B. N Kapoor in his cross examination 
has stated that Sri S. C Uppal and Kumari Rcnuka Dixit 
were junior to him. He expressed his ignorance on the 
point v hether they were permanent or temporary He even 
does not know 7 till what time they had worked in the 
bank. 

16. Similarly workman Si i Keshav Kumar Shukla, has de¬ 
posed in his cross examination that during the tenure of 
his employment at bank's Naveen Market Blanch Sri S. S. 
Maiwa and Sri D. K Sharara were working and they were 
junior to him He expressed his ignorance on the point 
whether these junior persons were working as temporary or 
permanent hands. Like Sri Kapoor he does not know whe¬ 
ther these two persons are still in the service of the bank. 

17 Both these workmen have admitted in their cross 
examination that the appointment to permanent post of 
clearks were made through Banking Service Recruitment 
Board even at the time when they were in f he employment 
of the Bank. 

18. Sri K. N. Soni, the authorised representative for the 
workmen, argued that the management indulged in unfair 
labour practice by appointing temporary employees for 
period’s not exceeding 85 days. I find no force in it. The 
workmen have not named persons either in their pleading 
or in their petition before the ALC (C) as to who were 
the persons who were so appointed by the management 
prior to their appointment. So I find no substance in this 
plea. 

19. Hence, hi view of the above discussion’s on the point 
of facts and law, I find no force in the contentions of Sri 
K. N. Soni., authorised lepresentative for the workmen Con¬ 
sequently the actio’ll of the management in terminating the 
services of the workmen and not considering them for fur¬ 
ther employment while engaging fresh hand’s is iustified. 
Accordingly the workmen are held entitled to no relief. 

20. All these above three reference are answered accord¬ 
ingly . 

21. Let a copv of this award be placed on the record of 
each connected file, 

ART AN DEV, Presiding Officer

S.O. 1793.—In pursuance of Section 17 of the Industiral 
Disputes Act, 1947 (14^of 1947)^ the Central Government 
hereby publishes the award of the Industrial Tribunal, Kan¬ 
pur as shown in the Annexure, in the industrial dispute bet¬ 
ween the etnloyers in relation to the management of North¬ 


ern Railway, Lucknow and their workmen, which was re¬ 
ceived by the Central Government on 10-6-92. 

[No. L-41012/26/92-JR(DL)(Pt.)] 
K.V.B. UNNY, Desk Officer 

ANNEXURE 

BEFORE SRI ARJAN DEV, PRESIDING OFFICER, 
CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL- 
CUM-LABOUR COURT, PANDU NAGAR, KANPUR 
Industrial Dispute No. 190 of 1991 
In the matter of dispute Detween : 

The Zonal Working President, U.R.K.U. 90/196 Roshan 
Bajaj Lane, Ganesh G'anj, Lucknow. 

AND 

The Chief Mechanical Engineer (W), Carriage and Wagor 
Workshop, Northern Rly. Alambagh, Lucknow. 

AWARD 

1. The Central Government, Ministry of Labour, vide its 
notification no. L-41012/26/92-LR. (D.U.) dated 14-11-91, 
ha 9 referred the following dispute for adjudication to this 
Tribunal:— 

Whether the Dy C.M.E, (W) C&W Shops Alambagh 
Northern Railway, Lucknow is justified in not pro¬ 
moting Sri Bhagwati Prasad as Bradma Operator 
Gr. I ? If not, what relief he is entitled to ? 

2. The instant case was fixed for filing of statement of 
claim on behalf of the Union on 6-4-92. Sri B. D. Tewari 
put in his appearance before the Tribunal in the above case 
in his capacity as Zonal Working President and submitted 
before the Tribunal that the benefit has already been received 
by the woikman for which the present case has been raised. 
He further requested before the Tribunal that the case may 
be closed. 

3. Sri Tewari also made a endorsement to the above 
effect on the order sheet of the case file on 6-4-92. 

4. So in view of the statements made by Sri Tewari on 
6-4-92 before the Tribunal, there appears no dispute bet¬ 
ween the parties. Therefore, I am inclined to ^ive a no claim 
av/ard in th case. 

5. Hence, in view of the facts and circumstances stated 
above, a no claim award is given in the case. 

6. Reference is answered accordingly. 

ARJAN DEV, Presiding Officer

[No. L-41012/6/88-DJI (B) (Pt.)l 
K. V. B. UNNY, Desk Officer 
ANNEXURE 

BEFORE SRI ARJAN DEV, PRESIDING OFFICER, 
CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL- 
CUM-LABOUR COURT, PANDU NAGAR KANPUR 

Industrial Dispute No. 208 of 1989 

In the mutter of dispute between : 

The Zonal Working President, 

Uttar Railway Karamchari Union, 

96[ 196 Roshan Bajaj Lane, 

Ganesh Ganj, Lucknow. 

AND 

Dy. C. M. E. Loco Workshop, 

Uttar Railway Charbagh, 

Lucknow. 

AWARD 

L The Central Government, Ministry of Labour, vide its 
notification no. L-41012|6|88-D.2(B) dt. 29-8-89 has referred 
the following dispute for adjudication to this Tribunal . 

Whether the Dy. C.M.E. Loco Workshop Charbagh 
Lucknow was justified in not granting npgradation 
as Bradma Operator Highly skilled Gr. II to 
Sh, lagdish Prasad Yadav w.e.f. 17-5-72 ? If not, 
to what relief the workman was entitled to ? 

2. The industrial dispute on behalf of the workman has 
been raised by Uttar Railway Karamchari Union (hereinafter 
referred to att Union), Lucknow, through its Zonal Working 
President. The Union has set up the case that Srj Jagdish 
Prasad Yadav workman joined us peon in Loco Workshop 
Charbagh Lucknow. On 26-6-88, he was promoted as 
Bradma Operator. Although he was due for promotion to 
Highly Skilled Gr. II on 17-5-72, this benefit was given to 
him on 1-3-85. The Union alleges that SjSri Justice Singh 
and Shambhu Dayal had been given promotion in superses¬ 
sion to the workman. In order to deprive the workman 
of highly skilled Gr. II his seniority was merged with the 
Light Machine Shop. He was proposed to be tested in' 
machinist grade when his due test was in the Right Fixing 
Section. Thus he was deprived of his due promotion in 
a malicious and discriminatory manner. 

3. The Union, has therefore, prayed that the workman 
be trated as a Gr. II Highly Skilled Bradma Operator (date 
not mentioned) and he was to be given arreais and revised 
settlement benefits. 

4. The case is contested by the railway management. The 
management plead that there were 4 posts of Bradma 
Operator Gr. Rs. 110-180 (RS) had been working in Rate 
Fixing Section against ex-cadre pottt and their seniority was 
maintained taking into consideration their appointment as 
skilled and also posting as Bradma Operator in Rate Fixing 
Section :— 


SI. Name 

No. 

T. No. 

Date of 
Biith 

Date of 
appointment 
as skilled 

1. Sh. J’isrice Singh 

OC-175 

23-1-3? 

22-4-54 

?, Sh. Y. K. Singh 

OG*47 

3-7-34 

6-12-60 

3. -Sh. R, K. Mis>-a

According to the management Sri Justice Singh OG-175 
was posted as Bradma Operator w.e.f. 16-10-65 and the 
workman OG-39 watt posted as Bradma Opeiator on 26-8-68. 
As regards S[Sri Y, K. Singh OG-42 and R. K. Mishra OG-36 
the management plead that they were also posted as Bradma 
Operator in Rate Fixing Section after Sri Justice Singh, but 
earlier to the workman. Lateron the post of Bradma Opera¬ 
tor Grade Rs. 110—-180 (RS) was in pin pointed as HSK Gr. I 
Bradma Operator Gr. Rs. 175-240 (RS) in March, 1972 
Sri Justice Singh, being the seniormost ’amongst all Bradma 
Operaor pin pointed post Gr. 175—240 (RS) and on 
beingfound suitable was promoted as HSK Gr. I Bradma 
Operator W’as trade tested the post of HSK Gr. I Bardma 
operator. As regards Sri Shambhu Dayal CG-177 it is 
pleaded by the management that he belongs to another trade 
i.e. Time Recorder, another ex-cadre post. Hence, the claim; 
of the workman hatt no connection whatsoever with the 
seniority over him. Lastly, the management plead that fa 
terms of Rly. Boards Letter No. E(NG)58.RS-6-26 dt. 22-4-63, 
the workman was allotted the trade of machinist as opted 
by him he was' assigned seniority against machinist Gr. LMS. 
Several times he was called for to appear for the trade 
test for the post of HSK Gr. II Machinist in LMS but every 
time he refused to appear in trade test. Thutt so far as this 
workman is concerned the Union has no case at all. 

5. In support of their case, the manageemnt have 
examined Sri A. R. Yadav Assistant Superintendent Establish¬ 
ment, and the Union has examined the workman in support 
of its case. 

6 . After hearing the authorised representatives for the 
parties and giving my anxioutt consideration to the evidence 
on record, I find that the Union has no case at all. The 
workman whose case has been espoused by the Union is 
not entitled to any relief. 

7. In para 2 and 3, of his statement in crostt examination, 
the workman has 'admitted that he is junior to all the three 
persons named by the management in para 3 of written 
statement. They are SjSri Justice Singh, Y. K. Singh and 
Sri R. K. Misra. 

8 . In para 8 of the written statement the management have 
pleaded that in terms of railway Roardtt letter dt. 22-4-63, 
the workman was allotted the trade of machinist as opted 
by him 'and he was assigned his seniority against machinist 
Grade in LMS. It is further pleaded in the ttaid para that 
several times the workman was called for to appear for 
the trade test for the post of HSK Gr. II Machinist in LMS 
but every time he refused to appear in the trade test. These 
facts have been corroborated by the management witness 
by means of para 8 of his affidavit. This fact that such an 
option was given by workman has been admitted by the 
workman in para 4 of his statement in cross examina¬ 
tion. In the same para at page 2 he h’as admitted that des¬ 
pite the fact that several times he was called to appear in 
the trade test, but he did not go and appear in the said test. 

9. So there remains the question of comparision with 
Sri Shambhu Dayal named by the Union in para 2 of the 
claim statement. About him in para T of the written state¬ 
ment the management have pleaded that Sri Shambhu Dayal 
is a time recorder which is an ex-cadre post and as such the 
question of examination of seniority vis-a-vis him and the 
workman does not arise. This fact has been corroborated by 
the management witness by means of facts deposed by himi 
in para 7 of he affidavit. 

10 . Thus we find that the Union has absolutely no case 
at ah to support his claim in respect of the workman. 

.11, Hence, the action of the Dy. C.M.E. Loco Shop 
Charbagh Lucknow in not granting upgradation of Bradma' 
Operator Highly Skilled Gr. II to the workman w.e.f. 17-5-721 
cannot be held as unjustified. Consequently, the workman! 
Union is entitled to no relief. 

12 . Reference is answered accordingly. 


ARJAN DEV, PRESIDING

they became entitled. 

5. The case is contested by the Rly. Administration. Fha 
management while admitting the fact that workman Sri 
Mishri Lai wns appointed on the post of casual khalasi 
under LO.L. Noither Railway Balamau on 21-10-77 plead 
that he had worked for a total period of 825 days upto 
14-12-84. The management deny that this workman ever 
acquired temporary status during the period 1540-79 to 
14-4-80. The details of his working during the said period 
are as follows :— 

15-10-79 to 1341-791 

154 L79 to 14-12-79 \ 91 days 

1542-79 to 144-80 f 

L2-80 to 144-80 J 

The management also plead hat the workman did not 
worked continuously upto 14-12-84. The total number of 
working days from 2140-77 to 14-12-84 are given as 835 
in para 3 of the written statement. According to the 
management this workman was not entitled to any notice 
under Ruie 149 of the Railway Establishment Code Vol. I, 
His name has however been entered in live register for 
future engagement as and when required as per para 2512 
of the Rly. Establishment Manual and P, S. No. 9195.

BETWEEN

Moradabad* 


AWARD 


1. The Central Government, Ministry of Labour, vide its 
notification no. L4-1Q11[6|89-D-2(B) dt, 30th October, 1989, 
has referred the following dispute for adjudication to this 
Tribunal :— 


Whether the Divisional Personnel Officer, Northern Rly. 
Moradabad and Asstt, Engineer Northern Rly, 
Hurdoi were justified in terminating the services of 
S|Sh. Mishri Lai and Bishram w.e.f. 14-2-84 & 
1441-84 respectively and also not giving them tem¬ 
porary status as per rule 149 of Rly. Estt. Code 
Vol. I ? If not, what relief the workmen were 
entitled to 7 


2 . The industrial dispute on behalf of the two workmen 
has been raised by Uttar Railway Karamchari Union (herein¬ 
after referred to as Union), through its Assistant General 
Secretary. 


3 , The case set up by the Union is that the workman 
Sri Mishri Lai was appointed on the post of Khnlash under 
IOW Balamau, on 2040-87 and he worked for 825 days 


6 . As regards the other workman Sri Bishram the manage¬ 
ment plead that he was never engaged as a casual labour| 
khalasi by the IOW Balamau, The case set up by the 
Union about him is therefore, without any basis, 

7. In this case no oral evidence has been led by the 
Union. On 12-2-91, Sri D. P. Awasthi, the auth. represen¬ 
tative for the Union submitted before the Tribunal that 
the Union had not to file any affidavit on behalf of the 
two workmen in the case. However, subsequently a copy 
of the causal labour card purporting to be of Sri Mishri 
Lai workman was filed by the Union. On the other hand 
in support of thelv case, the management examined Sri 
S. P. Jutla I.O.W. Northern Rly, Shahjahanpur The manage¬ 
ment, however, filed some documents summoned by the 
Union. 

8 . Theie are two workmen In this case, they are 
Sri Mishii Lai and Sri Eishram. So far as Sri Bishram is 
concerned no evidence has been adduced by the Union, 
However, by means of application dt. 16-3-90, the Union 
summoned paid vouchers of the period 15-1-80 to 144-80 
of Sri Mishri Lai and casual labour register to prove the 
working of Sri Bishram from the management. The appli¬ 
cation wm allowed on 25-5-90. 

9. On 10-9-90 the management moved an application 
stating that the paid vouchers of the period in question 
have been weeded out and to support it the management 
filed the copy of circular dated Feb. 1990 on the subject 
of weeding out of old and time barred records. The fact 
was not disputed from the side of the Union. The manage¬ 
ment filed photocopy of the casual labour legister on 
1040-90 pertaining to Sri Mishri Lai maintained rn the 
office of Assistant Engineer Hnrdoi. The formal proof of 
the document was waived by the authorised representative 
for the Union upon which the document was marked Ext* 
M1 On 31-7-91 the management moved an application 
stating that the original casual labour register had been

[«rnrli-^rrj(ii)] 


brought on that date but none appeared from (he s'dc of 
the l/noji. Thereafter none from the aide of the Union 
insisted upon filhigjbringing over again ot the said casual 
labour register. It is important to note that vide claim 
statement both the workmen are sakl to have worked under 
TO W. Bofempu. Had Sri Bishram worked as alleged in the 
claim stf. emcat and had his name been entered in the 
casual hbour register, the authorised representative for the 
Union m /1 have again sought an opportunity for summoning 
of the casual labour register over again. 

10. In 4 hc cheum^tance*, said above, I find absolutely no 
fo-ce in the case set up by the Union on behalf of Sri 
BisbttJn. In other words the Union has failed to prove 
that Sri Bahrain had ever worked under I.O.W. B'alamau 
as a Khalasi. 

11. Now we come to the case of Sri Mishri Lai. The 
Union simply filed the copy of casual labour card 
puipoiiuig to be of Sri Mishri Lai on 19-8-91. The Union 
made no attempt to prove it despite giving of several op¬ 
portunities. 

12. I may state here that it is not the Case of the Union 
that Sri Mishri Lai had worked for 240 days or more 
during the period of 12 months preceding the date of ter¬ 
mination of his services. From Ext. M.l referred to above 
it appears that during the period 15-12-83 to 14-12-84 he 
had worked continuously only for 153 days. So the provi¬ 
sions of section 25F I.D. Act are not attracted at all. 

13. Next question to be examined in connection with this 
workman is whether or not he had worked continuously 
from 15-10-79 to 14-4-80. Let us examine the entries ap¬ 
pearing in the copy of casual labour card filed by the Union. 
From the entry iL appears that during the period 20-10-77 
to 15-6-79 he had worked for 348 days only. Then at page 
5 of the top there appears entries of the period 14-4-81 to 
30-10~8L Against it total number of working days includ¬ 
ing 348 days of earlier working are shown as 408 days. 
Below 7 the entry for the period 14-4-81 to 30-10-81 at page 3 
appear entry of the period 5-9-79 to 4-12-80 and the total 
number of days after adding 408 days of earlier working has 
been shown as 676 days. How is it when entries of the 
period 14-4-81 to 30-10-81 appear above the entries of the 
period 5-9-79 to 4-12-80 against the entries of the period 
5-9-79 to 14-12-80. - There appear to be the signatures of 
some I.O.W. The management witness both in his affidavit 
and in his cross examination denied the signatures appearing 
against, these entries. He has even deposed that he was 
not posted as I.O.W. Balnmau during the said period. Then 
at page 6 at the top of first three entries are of 1983. They 
contradict the entries of 1981 appearing at page 5 Entries 
are :— 

Page (5) Page (6) 

14-4-81 to (4-5*81 15-4-81 to 14-5-81 

U 0-81 to 30-"0*$l 15-7-81 to 14-10-81 

15*10-81 to 30- 1 0-81 

Thus entries of casual labour card purporting to be of 
Sri Mishri Lai filed by the Union appears to me to be 
forged and fabricated. 

14. On the other hand, the working of this workman 
during the period 15-10-77 to 14-4-80 is shown in para 
2 of the written statement has been corroborated by the 
management witness. These entry show that there was 
only 91 days of continuous working from 15-10-79 14-1-80, 

In the circumstances he cannot be held to have acquired 
temporaty status. 

15. This workman having neither worked continuously for 
one year within the meaning of section 25 B I.D. Act, 
during the period of 12 months preceding the date of termi¬ 
nation of his services and having not acquired temporary 
status was not entitled to any notice. 

16. Thus the Union failed in proving its case in respect 
of the two workmen named in the reference order. Conse- 
quentlv the reference is answered against the Union/workmen. 

17. Reference is answered accordingly.

In the matter of an industrial dispute under Section 10(l)(d) 
of the I. D. Act, 1947 

Reference No. 315 of 1987 

PARTIES : 

Employer* in 1 elation to the management of Mahuda 
Area No II of M/s. B.C.C. Ltd. and their work* 
men. 

APPEARANCES : 

On behalf of the worlmen.—Shri J. D. Lall, Advocate. 

On behalf c£ the employers.—Shri B. Joshi, Advocate. 
STATE : fcihat INDUSTRY : Goal 

Dated, Dhanbad, the May, 1992 
AWARD 

The Govt, oi India, Ministry of Labour in exercise of the 
powers conferred on them under Section 10(l)(d) of the I. B. 
Act., 1947 has referred the following dispute to this Tribunal 
for adjudication vide their Order No, L-24Q12(89)/87-D.IV(B), 
dated, the 4th December, 1987. 

SCHEDULE 

“Whether the action of the Management of Mohuda 
Area No. II of M/s. B.C.C. Ltd. in not referring 
the case of Sri D. C. Gayali Accounts Clerk to 
Medical Board for assessing his actual age in view, 
of variations in the different records of Rhatdih and 
Mohid area collieries, is justified ? If not, to what 
relief the workman concerned is entitled ?” 

2. Shri D. C. Gayali, the concerned workman was working 
as Accounts Clerk in Area office of the General Manager, 
BCCL Mohuda Area No. HI. As per statement of claims the 
concerned workman stated that in the record of the BCCL 
his date of birth was recorded as 1-4-1931 but all of a sud¬ 
den he was served with a notice dated J7-7-1986 from the 
General Manager, Mohuda Area that he would superannuate 
with effect from 28-8-86 on attainment of 60 years of age* 
The concerned workman immediately protested and submitted 
a written representation that according to the identity card 
and other records of the colliery his date of birth was 
1-4-1931 and as such he would superannuate on 1-4 91 and 
not with effect from 28-8-86 as stated fcy the management. 
The workman submitted various documents ‘n support of his 
claim such as identity card, affidavit and middle school exa- 
mination cert ficate to the management but it was all fruitless. 

3. During the conciliat on proceeding the management did 
not file any document showing that the concerned woikmun 
was to superannuate on 28-8416. No document such as Foim 
B Register was produced before the Conciliation Officer in 
support of their contention. 

4. The concerned workman furthei contended that ac¬ 
cording to the circular dated 25-4-1988 any dispute .re¬ 
garding the age/date of birth of an employee* of Coal Mines 
is to be resolved in accordance with the guidelines mentioned 
in the circular. As per gmdelnes the date of birrhas ie- 
corded in th$ middle school certificate issue<Tl>y" the depart¬ 
ment of Public instructions should be accepted as correct. 
The concerned workman contended that according tc the 
middle school his date of birth has been recorded as 
1-4-1931 and that being the pos tiou his superannuation with 
effect from 28-8-86 wa.s illegal and malafide. Accoidinglv it 
has been prayed that the management of Mohuda area be 
directed to accept the date of birth as recorded in the 
Middle School certifr ate of the concerned workman and 
also in the identity card issued by the management as the 
actual date of birth. It has also been prayed that the 
management be direc ed to re nstate the concerned work¬ 
man to his original witn continuity of service and full back 
wages for the idle period. 

5. The management on the other hand has stated that 
at the time of appointment of the concerned wotkman at 
Bhatdih colliery he had submitted his particulars including 1 
the date of birth .which were entered in the statutory register. 
He had declared his date of birth as 2 8 1924 which were 


recorded in Form B Register of Bhatdih colliery and he had 
also put his signature m token of its being correct. It was 
further stated that on 21-5-74, the concerned workman was 
transferred to Mohuda area office and after joining the area 
officer he declared his another date of birth which was 
recorded in the Form B Register. He took undue advantage 
of changing his date of birth because oi his transfer to a 
new' place. The management also stated that variation in 
age was deliberately created by the concerned workman with 
ulterior motive in Form B Register of Mohuda area and 
there was m> glaring mistake in his date of birth at Bhatdih 
colliery which was the place o£ his init al appointment. In 
this way it was contended that the concerned workman has 
got no claim, 

6 . It may be noted at the very outset that during the 
pendency of the reference Shri Gayali, the concerned work¬ 
man died. A petition was filed on behalf of the management 
that after the death of the concerned workman no dispute 
subsists in terms of the teference and accordingly it was 

urged to pass necessary order. It was also contended that the 
Tribunal cannot go beyond the terms of reference. On the 
other hand the learned counsel for the workmen submitted 
that a dispute does not necessarilv cease to be an industrial 
dispute upon the death of the wo i km an concerned. 

7. In the circumstances of the case the question arises 
for our conideration is whethe^ the junsdict'on of any 
Industrial Tribunal to proceed with the adjudication of any 
industrial dispute ceases with the death of the concerned 
workman. 

8 . I have already heard both the sides at length. The 
learned counsel for the workman submitted that the reference 
was not in consonance with the relief of the concerned 
workman and the Tribunal has got power to go beyond 
the reference and decide the matter leaving no scope for 
any grievance from any side. Placing reliance upon the autho¬ 
rity reported in Supreme Court Labour Judgement 1950- 
1967 at page 3139 it was submitted that an oider of refe¬ 
rence has tily drawn or drawn 'n a casual manner often 
gives rise to unnecessary disputes and thereby prolongs 
the life of industrial adjudication which must always be 
avoided/ Even so, when the question of this kind is raised 
before the Courts, the Courts must attempt to construe the 
reference not too technically or n a pedantic manner, but 
fairly and reasonably. The learned counsel in his continued 
argument urged that the concerned workman had necessary 
certificate i.e. Identity card and Middle School Certificate 
to prove that he was born on 1-4-1931 but the management 
did not consider those documents. Further reliance was 
placed upon the authority reported in 1968 Lab I. C. 515 
equivalent to AIR 1968. Patna page 135 wherein their 
Lordships held that on the death of the workman concerned 
the industrial dispute may not necessarily and I have exami¬ 
ned the authority reported in SCLJ as referred to above 
wherein a complicated question of facts rclatng to the lock 
out Dr closure was involved. But here in the instant case 
the subject matter is quite different and I do not think 
that the facts and question involved in the authority reported 
above can be applied in the instant case. As regards another 
authority as reported iu 1968 Lab I.C. I find that the dispute 
which was referred for adjudication to the Tribunal was 
with respect to the pay and emoluments to which Shri 
Abhiram Jha was entitled. The Tribunal would naturally 
have had to consider whether the duties and function which 
Shri Jha discharged were those of sub-editors as maintained 
by the management or those of Chief Sub-Editor as claimed 
on behalf of the deceased. The Hon'ble Court while laying 
down the principles expressed their v ew as follows:— 

“Therefore it cannot be laid down as universal proposi¬ 
tion that a dispute ceased to be an industrial 
dispute upon the deatn of the woikman concerned. 
The question in each case depends upon the nature 
or the character of the dispute.” 

One ^example was also quoted by their Lordships which 
was as follows:— 

“For example, where a dispure arises with regard to 
the status of a workman, -and such a workman 
dies, the other workman or their Union mav still 
be interested in adjudication of the status of the 
deceased workman because that may affect the sta¬ 
tus of his sucressor-‘n-office. In that ca c e the status 
of Shri Abhiram Jha was undoubtedly a question 
in which he was personally interested. Rut it was- 
also a question in which his successors-in-office 



2956 THE GAZETTE OF INDJA : JULY 4, 


would be inteiested, Whoever is or has been appoint¬ 
ed to djscharge the identical duties and function' 
of Shri Abhiiam Jha, is obviously inteiested in 
the adjudication of the quest'on w nether Shri 
Abhiram Jha was a sub-Edi or oi a Chief snt- 
Editor of the Newspnpei." 

9 . Here in the instant ease wc find that the Issue 
involved in this reference is not indenlicnl to the example 
m set out by tber Lordship. In the present case the question 
was that the concerned woikman should have been refer¬ 
red to the Medical Hoard f vr assessing his actual age jn 
view of the variation in lh<" different icauds of Bhatdih 
colliery and Mghuda are i blit the same was lefused by 
the management. 

10. That was a quc,rion tlu decision of which was not 
going to affect the uicccs^oi-in-office oi Shn Ga}ali. That 
was purely a petsoual matter. Nobody had to do anything 
with the age of the concerned workman In the ciicum- 
stances 1 ain to hold that the authority cited above cannot 
help the concerned workman. 

11. The learned counsel for the management has relied 
upon the same authouty reported in SOL7 a* page 2370 
wherin their Loidships held as follows:— 

“The Tiibunal must confine its adjudication to the 
points of dispu e referred and the matter incidental 
thereto. In otherwords, the Tiibunal is not fice 
to enarge the dispute refened to it but must con¬ 
fine its attention to the points specifically mention¬ 
ed and anything which is inciden al thereto." 

12 . 1 have examined this aspect of the matter as also the re¬ 

cent circular No. 01L|NCWAIII|Instiuclion No, 7b 8S|ltt5 
dated 25-4-1988. A photo copy of the circular is on the 
record. In the ciicular noted above it has been slated in 

para-6 that the earlier Implementation Instruction No. 37 

dated 5.2.1981 haa been revised and the same is enclosed ns 
annexure-I. In nnnexure-I the guideline and the procedure 

has been noted for detcimination of the age at various 
stages. In clause (b) of Implementation Instruction No, 76 
it has been stated that in case of existing employees 
Matriculation certificate or H.3. cert dentes issued by the 
recognised university oi hoard or middle pass ceitificate 
issued by the board of educat on or the department of

7. Shri B. loshi, learned Advocate for the management, 
ha^ contended that normally retrospective operation is not 
given to statute so as to impair an existing right or obligation, 
otherwise lhan as regards matter of procedure but when (he 
language of statute is plainly retrospective, it may be inter¬ 
preted as Mich. 

8 Shri 1 alit Burman, authorised representative of the 
union, has contended that every statute which taken away or 
implies vested rights acquired under existing laws, or creates 
a new obligation must be presumed to be intended not to 
have a retrosepetive operation In order to appreciate and 
deckle the contention of the parties, it is necessary to deal 
with certain admitted fuels and legal position. 

9. Admittedly, the present industrial disptue has been 
raised bv the conccmed workmen/union with reference to the 
establishment of ihc management of Bhowra Coke Plant of 
M/} R.C.C 1 td which was nationalised with effect from the 
appointed day, ie 1-5-1972 under the provisions of Coking 
Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1972. Section 17(1) and 
(2) of the said Act provide as follows : 

14 17(1) Every person who h a workmen within the 
meaning of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, and 
has been, immediately before the appointed day. in 
the employment of a Coking Coal Mine or Coke 
Oven Plant, shall become on and frrrni the appointed 
day, an employee of the Central Government, on 
ns the case may' be, of the Government Company 
in which the right, title and inteiest of such mine 
oi nlant have vested under this Act and shall hold 
office or service in the coking coal mine or coke

been admissible to him if the rights in relation tp 
such coking coal mine or coke oven plant had not

Government or Government company, as the case

hts employment in such coking coal mine or coke 
o\en plant is duly terminated or until his remunera¬

(2) The CVnlral Government oi the Government com¬

The implication of Sections 9 and 17 of Coking Coal Mines 
(Nationalisation) Act, 1972 fell for determination before 
the Hon'ble Supreme Court in the case of workmen con¬ 
cerned represented by Rihur Colliery Kamgar Union Vs. 
M/s, BCC Ltd. and others reported in S.C.LJ, (Vol-15) 
(1978-79) page 27 fSC). The Honblc Court has decide 
as follows : 

“Section J7 is a special provision relating to uoikmen 
and their continuance in service notwithstanding the 
transfer from private ownership to the Cential Go¬ 
vernment or Government company. Thi 3 is a statu- 
lory protection for the workmen and is express, 
explicit and mandatory. Every person who 13 a 
workman within the meaning of the Industrial Dis¬ 
putes Act, 1947, and has been, immediately befoie 
the appointed da>. in the empio>nient of a mine, 
shall become an emplo>ee of the Government or 
the Government company and continue to do so 
ns laid down m Section 17, A ‘workman’ is defined 
ip the Industrial Disputes Act to mean ‘any person 
employed in any industry (we omit the unnecessary 
words) and includes any such peison who has been 
dismissed and whose dismissal has led to a dispute’, 
It is perfectly plain that the 40 workmen who were 
dismissed and whose dismissal led to the industrial 
dispute are ‘workmen’ within the meaning of Sec¬ 
tion 17(1) of the Act. Irrefutably follows the 
inference that they are workmen entitled to conti¬ 
nuance in service as provided for in Section 17, It 
is not open to any one to contend that because thev 
had been wrongfully dismissed and, therefore, arc 
not physically on the rolls on the date of the take¬ 
over Ihcv are not legally workmen under the new 
ow'nei, The subtle eve of the law transcends exis¬ 
tence on the gross level. The statutory continuity 
of service cannot be breached by lhe wrongful dis¬ 
missal of the prior employer/’ 

10 . One salient fact is not disputed ; there was no industrial 
dispute pending on the appointed day over denial of employ¬ 
ment to the concerned workmen by the management. The 
claim of the concerned woikmcn is based on Section 17(1) of 
the Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1972, The 
Coal Mines Nationalisation Laws (Amendment) Act, 1986 
has substituted Section 17 of the Coking Coal Mines (Nationali¬ 
sation) Act. 1972 by a new Section. 'I he relevant section 
reads as follows : 

“17 Notwiihstanding anything contained in the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947 or in anv other law for the time 
being in force, the services of any officer or other 
employee employed in a coking coal mine or coke 
oven plani shall be liable to be transferred to any 
other coking coal mine or coke oven plant and 
such transfer shall not entitle such officer or other 
employee to anv compensation under this Act or any 
other law for the time being in force and no such 
claim be entertained by any court, tribunal or other 
authority .* 1 

Ghowra Coke Plant, as 1 have pointed out before, is a 
coking coal plant; it was nationalised with effect fiom 1-5-72 

11. Shii R roO-ii learned Advocate foi the management, 
has submitted thru the pmvsion ot Section 17 of the Coking 
toal Aline t Nidloirtli iiiiou ) \ci and Section 14 of the 
Coal Mines NimomlUation) Act are similar* The position 
is really so. He has further submitted that bv substitution of 


these two sections in the Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisation) 
Act and Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act by a similar pro¬ 
vision in the Coal Mines Nationalisation Laws (Amendment) 
Act, J986 us mentioned before, the concerned woikmen have 
no longer any right to claim employment under the establish¬ 
ment of M, s, H.C.C. Ltd. He has cited the decision of the 
Hon’ble Patna High Court in the matter of employment in 
1 elation to the management of Ramkanali Colliery of M/s. 
B.C.C. Ltd. Vs. Their Workmen represented by Rashtriya 
Collieiy Mazdoor Snngh and another. The relevant portion 
of the decision of Hon’ble Court reads as follows : 

v The amendment of any statute may be by adding or 
introducing new provision or by deleting it or by 
substituting It. Substitution of statute by a new 
provision is also an amendment of the original 
statute. It must, therefore, be held that although 
in the Amendment Act of 1986 so far original Sec¬ 
tion 14 was concerned, it was stated that that shall 
be substituted, it amounted to amendment of original 
Section 14 by substituting it by the provisions intro¬ 
duced by the Amendment Act of 1986. Respondent 
No, 2 was clearly in error in law in holding that a.s 
in the case Section 14 of 1973 Act was a cage of 
substitution, it came into force on 7th October, 1986, 
It must be held that the substituted Section 14 came 
ino force with effect from 1-5-73 and as the four 
workmen were not in the employment of the coal 
mine in question on the appointed date i.e, T5-73, 
respondent No. 2 could not have directed for their 
reinstatement/* 

The Hon'ble Court has rendered the judgement in connec¬ 
tion with provision of Coat Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 
1973 Rut I have already pointed out that the relevant provi¬ 
sions in Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act and Coal 
Mines (Nationalisation) Act are similar. So, the decisions of 
rhe Hon’ble Court as mentioned above remains 4 as such valid 
m 1 elation to cases under Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisa¬ 
tion) Act, 1973. 

Slui Joshi has further submitted that if the language of 
any statute is plainly retrospective, it niu^t be so interpreted 
although h might impair an existing right or obligation. In 
support of this contention he has teferred to Maxwell on the 
Tnterpietation of Statute (Eleventh Edition) page 205. 

12. Section 17 of the Coking Coal Mines ^Nationalisation) 
Act has given stalutoiy protection for continuity of service 
to the workman. It cannot be regarded m a vested right. 
Vested right is not contingent upon anything : it is settled 
and absolute. Now the statutory protection given by a 
stilluto can be withdrawn by another statute, provided there 
exists intendment therefor. The principle is well settled that 
retrospective operation is not to be given to a statute so as 
to impaii an existing right. This is the general rule. But 
the Legislature is competent to frame a statute so as to have 
retrospective operation either by clearly expressing such inten¬ 
tion or by necessaiy and distinct intendmeht. This being 
< 0 . it is necessary hcie to consider the piovision of the Coal 
Mines Nationalisation Laws (Amendment) Act, 1986 and to 
ascertain if there is any such intendment in (bis Ad to repeal 
the provision of Section 17 of the Cok*ny f ( \il Mine, (Na¬ 
tionalisation) Act and Section 14 oi the Coal^ Mines 
(Nationalisaiion) Act with retrospective effect. Section 1(2) 
of l.be said Act reads as follows : 

“Save as otherwise expressly provided, the amendments 
to the Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 
1972 shall be deemed to have come into force on 
the Dt day of Mav, 1972 and the amendments to 
the Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1973 shall 
be deemed to have come into force on the 1 st dav 
of May, 1973, and the remaining provisions of this 
Act shall be deemed to have come into force on the 
7th dav of October, 1986/’ 

This sub-section envisages that amendment to Coking Coal 
Mines (Nationalisation) Act and Coal Mines (Nationalisation) 
Act shall be deemed to have come into force on 1-5-72 and 
1 . 5,73 Tc«pcct(\ elv and the remaining provisions of the Amend- 
meni Aci shall t omfr imo lorn* on 7-10-1986, Theratter 
the Amendment Act divides different sections of that Act as 
‘amendment* bv side notes and different sections as ‘snbstitu^ 




[wr-r II— « j(ii)] 


wTWa: 4, lgai/swr? 1 j, 1914] 


2959 


tion* bj side notes a -3 well as by incorporating such ‘substitu¬ 
tion* m the core of substituted section. Thus the scheme of 
the Amendment Act is dearly to recognise some sections of 
that Act as ‘amendment’ and some sections as ‘substitution’. 
Section 17 of the Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act 
and Section 14 of the Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act have 
been substituted by new sections. Such being the position, 
the intendment of the legislature, in my view was to substi¬ 
tute these two sections of Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisa¬ 
tion) Act and Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act by new 
sections and thereby to give effect to the substituted sections 
with effect from 7-10-86 and not from 1-5-72 or 1-5-73. 
But the Hon Ne Patna High Court has decided that amend¬ 
ment includes substitution and so the substituted sections 
shall be deemd to have come into effect on 1-5-72 or 1-5-73 
as the case may be. Although there appears to have scope is 
differ with the Hon’ble Court on this point, this Tribunal is 
bound by the decision of the Hon’ble Court. Hence, it shall 
be concluded that the concerned workman cannot vindicate 
their right in the present reference case on the strength ct 
provision of Section 17 of the Coking Coal Mines (Nationali¬ 
sation) Act, 1972. 

13. 1 have considered the piovisions of Section 18(3) of 
the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 to ascertain if the concerned 
workmen can vindicate their right under the provision afore¬ 
said. The application of Section 18(3) is depended upon a 
settlement arrived at in the corn sc of conciliation proceeding 
under the Act or Arbitration Award or an Award of a Labour 
Court, Tribunal or National Tribunal which has become en¬ 
forceable. Such settlement or Arbitration Award or Award 
shall be binding on — 

(a) all parties to the industrial dispute ; 

(b) all other parties summoned to appear in the procee¬ 

ding as rarties to the dispute ; 

'c) where a party referred to in clause (a) or clause (b) 
is an employee, his heii, successor or assigns in 
respect of the establishment to which the dispute 
relates ; 

(d) where a party tefened to in clause (a) or clause (b) 
is composed of workmen, all persons who were 
employed in the establishment or part of the esta¬ 
blishment, ns the case may be, to which the dispute 
relates on the date of the dispute and all persons 
who subsequently become employed in that establish¬ 
ment or part.” 

In the present case the pleadings of the parties do not 
disclose that liny settlement was arrived at during the course 
of conciliation proceeding or that an Arbitration Award or 
an Award of Labour Court, Tribunal or National Tribunal 
which has become enforceable was passed. Hence, there is 
no scope to hold that the concerned workmen can vindicate 
their rights under the provisions of Section 18(3) of the 
Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, 

14. Accordingly, the following award is rendered— 

the present reference ca^e is not maintainable. Conse¬ 
quently, it is held that the action of the management 
of Bharat Coking Coal Ltd. in denying employment 
to Shri Marayan S’en and 89 others is justified. 

In the circumstances of the case, I award no cost. 

This is my award. 

S K. MITRA, Presiding Officer

He was given full oppo“‘nmtv to cross examine the manage¬

ano to give his own statement. 

The enquiry was conducted in conformance to the principles 
of natural justice The Enqu'-y Office** submitted his xepojt

The concerned workman was a permanent woikman of

2, The case of the concerned workman, Sri Bhag¬ 
wat Dusadh, briefly stated, is that he worked in Amla- 
bad Colliciy of M|s. B.C.C. Ltd. as Miner)Loader from 1 
18-1-62 to March, 1971 continuously. He was on

he was dismissed from service lor misconduct of rio¬ 
tous and disorderly behaviour. Anyway, the conci¬ 
liation proceeding ended in a failure and the appro¬ 
priate Government has referred the dispute for ad¬ 
judication by this Tribnal. 

3. The management has been contesting the case; 
in doing so, it has raised two-fold defence: both tech¬ 
nical and substantive. The technical defence raised 
by the management is that the concerned workman 
was not on the roll of the colliery at the time of take 
over or the colliery by the Central Government on 
17-10-1971 and hence, he can not claim employment 
under the present management Another point rais¬ 
ed by he management is that after coming into force 
of the Coal Mines Nationalisation Laws (Amend¬ 
ment) Acl 1986 which became effective from 
15-12-1986 no workman of the erstwhile employers 
ot the colliery can claim employment under the present 
Government company which became the owner of the 
colliery by virtue of Nationalisation of Coal Mines by 
the Act of' Parliament. In other words, the conten¬ 
tion of the management is that the claim of the con¬ 
cerned workman for employment under the present 
management is not maintainable under the law. 

4 At the instance of the management, the ques¬ 
tion of maintainability of present industrial dispute 
has been taken up as a preliminary issue. Hence, 
ihe substantive defence raised by the management 
does not squarely fall lor decision while considering 
the preliminary is^ue. 

5. Shri J. D, Lai Earned Advcate for the concern¬ 
ed workman, has contended that it has become a 
fashion will the management to raise preliminary ob¬ 
jection as to the maintainability of the industrial dis¬ 
putes in order to delay the hearing of such disputes 
on merik I consider that the contention of Shri 
Lai is not without any merit. Indeed the Hon’ble 
Supreme Court ha; deprecated the tendency of hear¬ 
ing of preliminaiv objection raised by the manage- 
nvn*. Th'* decisions rejwrfcd in (1) 1983 (II) LLJ. 
429 (S. K. Vermu Vs; Mahesh Chandra and another) 
and (21 1983 Lab. 1. C. 1629 (D.P. Maheshwari 
Vs, Delhi Administration and others) may be cited 
in support of this position. But the Hon’ble Court 



2 9j1 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: JULY 4, 


has not put any embaigo on the Tribunal on the 
hearing of preliminary issue which is considered vital 
and cuts to the very root of the case* In the present 
case, the management has taken the plea that 
the claim of the concerned workman for 
employment is not sustainable in view of the Coal 
Mines Nationalisation Law (Amendment) Act, 1986. 

In other words, according to the management, the 
Amendment Act cuts to the very root of the case 
ot the concerned workman. This being so, 1 over¬ 
rule the objection raised by Sbri Lai against hearing 
the preliminary issue, 

6. Shri B. Joshi, learned Advocate for the manage¬ 
ment, has contended that normally a retrospective 
operation is not given to statute so as to impair an 
existing right or obligation, otherwise than as regards 
matter of proceduie, but when the language of statute 
is plainly retiospcctive, it may be interpreted as such. 

7. Shri J. D. Lal t learned Advocate for the con¬ 
cerned workman, has contended that every statute 
which taken away or impires vested rights acquired 
under existing laws, or ci cates a new obligation must 
be presumed to be intended not to have a retrospec¬ 
tive operation. In order to appreciate and decide the 
contention of the parties, it is nccressaiy to deal with 
certain admitted facts and legal position. 

8* Admittedly, the present industrial dispute has 
been raised by the concerned workman|uuion with 
reference to the establishment of the management of 
Amlabad Colliery of Bhowra Area (Area No. XI) of 
M[s. B.C.C. Ltd. Amlabad Colliery is a Coking Coal 
Mines which was nationalised with effect from the 
appointed day, i.c. 1-5-1972 under the provisions of 
Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Adi, 1972. 
Section 17(1) & (2) of the said Act provide as 
follows : 

“17. (1) Every poison who is a workman within 
the meaning of the Industrial Dispute Act, 
1947, and has been, immediately before 
the appointed day, in th'e employment of a 
Coking Coal Mine or Coke Oven Plant, shall 
become on and from the appointed day, an 
employee of the Central Government, or, 
as the case may be, of the Government 
company in which the right, title and in¬ 
terest of such mine or plant have vested 
under this Act and shall hold office or 
service in the coking coal mine or coke oven 
plant, as the case may be, on tile same terms 
and conditions and with the same rights to 
pension, giatuity and other matters as would 
have been admissible to him if the rightsr 
in relation to such coking coal mine or 
coke oven plant had not been transferred 
to, and vested in the Cenral Government or 
Government company, as the case may be, 
and continue to do so unless and until his 
employment in such coking coal mine or 
coke oven plant is duly terminated or until 
his remunerarion, terms and conditions of 
employment are duly altered, by the Central 
Government or the Government company. 

(2) The Central Government or the Gov- 
eminent companv in which the rights, title 
and interest in relation to a coking coal mine 
or coke oven plant have vested, may emp-

loy, on mutually acceptable terms and con¬ 
ditions, any pet son who is not a workman 
within lhe meaning of the Industrial Disputes 
Act, 1947, und who has been, immediate¬

The implication of Sections 9 and 17 of Coking 
Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1972 fell for 
determination beiore the HctTble Supreme Court m 
the case of woikmcn concerned represented by Bihar 
Colliciy Kamgar Union Vs. M s. B.C.C Ltd. and 
other, reported in S.C L.J (Vol. 15) (1978-79) 

page 27 (SC). The Horfble Court has edcided as 
follows : 

“Section 17 is a special piovision relating to 
workmen and their continuance in service 
notwithstanding the transfer' from private 
ownership to lhe Central Government or 
Government company. This is a statutory 
protection for the workmen and is express, 
explicit and mandatory. Every person who 
is a workman within the meaning of the 
Industrial, Disputes Act, 1947, and has been, 
immediately before the appointed day, in 
the employment of a nunc, shall become an 
employee of the Government or the Govern¬ 
ment Company and continue to do so as 
laid down in Section 17. A ‘woikmaiL is 
defined in the Industrial Disputes Act to 
mean 'any person employed in any industry 
(we omit the unnecessary words) and in¬ 
cludes any such person who has been dis¬ 
missed and whose dismissal has led to a 
dispute’. It ib perfectly plain that the 40 
workmen who were dismissed and whose 
dismissal led to the industiial dispute are 
‘workmen’ within the meaning of Section 
17(1) of the Act. Irrefutably follows the 
inference lhat they are workmen entitled to 
continuance in service as provided for in 
Section 17. Tt is not open to any one to 
contend that because they had been wrong¬ 
fully dismissed and, theiefore, arc not phy¬ 
sically on the rolls on the date of the take¬ 
over, they are not legally workmen under 
the new owner. The subtle eye of the law 
transcends existence on the gross level The 
statutory continuity ol service cannot be 
bleached by the wrongful dismissal ot the 
prior employer.” 

9 In the present case the management has al¬ 
leged tint the concerned workman was dismissed 
from service for commission of serious" misconducts 
of vHen\ riotous and disorderly behaviours in 
March, 1971 after the charges against him were 
established. The concerned workman has stated in 
his written statement that he proceeded on leave 
sometime in March, 1971 because of sickness of

his mother at his village home and when he re¬

gement not were his services even terminated by

ot evidence as to whether the concerned workman

whether he was not allowed to join his duty by the
