10. Sini B. Joshi, learned Advocate for the mana¬ 
gement, has submitted that the provision of Section 
17 of the Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Ae f 
and Section 14 of the Coal Mines (Nationalisation) 
Act are similar. The position is really so. He has 
further submitted Oiat by substitution of these two 
sections in the Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisation) 
Act and Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act by a simi¬ 
lar provision in the Coal Mines Nationalisation 
Laws (Amendment) Act, 1986 as mentioned before, 
the concerned workman has no longer any right to 
claim employment under the establishment oT M|s. 
B.C.C. Ltd He has eked the decision of the Hon'ble 
Patna High Court in the matter of employment in 
relation to the management of Ramkaitali Coilieiy of 
M|s. B C.C. Ltd. Vs. Their Workmen represented 
by Rashtriya CoJlieiy Mazdoor Sangh and another. 
The relevant portion of the decision of Hon'bJc 
Court leads as follows : 

“The amendment of anv statute may be by 
adding or introducing new provision or by 
deleting it or by substituting it. Substitution 
of satabite by a rVew provision is also an 
amendment of the original sfatute. Tt 
must, therefore, be held that although 
in the Amendment Act of 1986 


so far original section 14 was concerned, 
it was stated that that shall be substitu¬ 
ted, it amounted to amendment of 
original section 14 by subs ituting it by 
the provisions introduced by the Amend¬ 
ment Act of 1986. Respondent No 2 was 
clearly in error in law in holding that as 
in the case section J4 of 1973 Act was a 
case of substitution, it came into force on 
7th October, 1986. It must be held that 
the substituted section 14 came into force 
with effect lrom 1-5-73 and as th’o four 
woikmcn were not in the employment of 
the coal mine in question on the appointed 
date i.c. 1 5-73, icspondcnt no. 2 could 
lot have directed for th-ir reinstatement.” 

The Hon'blc Court has lendeieo the judgement in 
eonnec ion with provision of Coal Mines (Nationals 
satica) Act, 1973. But !l have already pointed out 
(hat the relevant provisions m Coking Coal Mines 
(Nationalisation) Act and Coal Mines (Nationali- 
ation) Act are similar. So the decisions of he 
Hon'ble Court as mentioned above remains as much 
valid in relation to cases under Coal Mip£s (Na¬ 
tionalisation) Act as to caves under Coking Coal 
Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1973. 

Shri Joshi has further submitted that if the lan¬ 
guage of any statute is plainly retrospective, it invest 
be so interpreted although it might impair and 
existing right or obligation. In support ot this* con¬ 
tention he has ldeiied h) Maxwell on the Interpreta¬ 
tion of Statute (Eleventh Edition) page 205. 

II. Shri J D, Lai, learned Advocate for the con¬ 
cerned workman, has counlcied the contention of 
Shri Joshi by j el erring to the provision of Section 
6(1) & (c) of the Cicneial Clause Act, 1897, Sec¬ 
tion 6 of the General Clauses Act underlines 
the fact ot repeal of any statute. The relevant por¬ 
tion of See 6 of the said Ac’ is reproduced hercin- 
below; 

“Where this Act, or any Central Act or Regu¬ 
lation made after commencement of this 
Act, repeals any enactmoit hithet.o made 
or hereafter to be made, then, unless a 
different intention appears, the rencal shall 
not— 

(a) . 

(b) effect (a) the pievicus operation of any 
enactment so repealed or anything duly 
done or sulleied thereunder; or 

(c) effect any right, privilege, obligation or 
liability acquired, accrrced, or incurred 
under anv enactment so repealed/’ 

Shii Lai has submitted that the concerned work¬ 
man acquired a right to employment under the new 
management consequent upon the nationalisation of 
Cokina Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act. Accord¬ 
ing to him, this right to employment under the new 
employer cannot be taken away by the Amendment 
Act. He has referred me the decision reported in 
ATR 1969(SC i 701 (T.S. Baliah Vs. T.S. Ranga- 
chaii. Income Tax Officer, Ccnha] Circle VI, 





2^64 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: JULY 4, 1992/ASADHA 13. 1514 [Part II—S E r. 3(ii)l 


Madras) in support of this contention. He has criti¬ 
cised the judgement of Hon'ble Patna High Court by 
stating that the Hon’ble Couit did not consider the 
judicial precedent in the matter and so the decision 
rendered by Hon’ble Patna High Court without 
considering the judicial precedent cannot be a bind¬ 
ing precedent. In this connection he has cited the 
case reported in 1987 Lab. LC. 335 (N.N. Nivani& 
etc. Vs. State of Karntaka and another). He has 
also cited the decision of Madras High Court re¬ 
ported in 1985 Lab. LC. 917 (Syed Mohidccn Vs. 
Government of Tamil Nadu and another) in order 
to emphasise the fact; that the decision per incuriam 
cannot be followed as a binding precedent. 

12. Section 17 of the Coking Coal Mines (Nationa¬ 
lisation) Act has given statutory protection 
for continuity of service to the workman. It cannot 
be regarded us a vested right. Vested right is not 
contingent upon anything ; it is settled and absolute. 
Now the statutory protec ion given by a statute can 
be withdrawn by another statute, provided there 
exists intendment therefor. The principle is well set¬ 
tled that a retrospective) operation is not to be given 
to a statute so as to impair an existing right. This 
is the general julo. But the Legislature is compe¬ 
tent to frame a statute so as to have retrospective 
operation cither by clearly expressing such intention 
or by necessary and distinct intendment. This being 
so, it is necessury here to consider the provision of 
the Coal Mines Nationalisation laws (Amend¬ 
ment) Act, 1986 and to ascertain if there is any 
such in endment in this Act to repeal the provision 
of Section 17 of the Cokin/j Coal Mines (Nationa¬ 
lisation) Act and Section 14 of the Coal Mines 
(Nationalisation) Act with retrospective effect. Section 
1(2) of the said Act reads as follows : 

“Save as otherwise expressly provided, the 
amendments to the Coking Coal Mines 
(Nationalisation ) Act, 1972 shall be deemed 
to have come into force on the 1st day 
of May, 1972 and the amendments to the 
Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act , 1973 
shall be deemed to have come into force 
on the 1st day of May, 1973, and the re¬ 
maining provisions of this Act shall be 
doemed to have come into force on the 
7th day of October, 1986.“ 

This sub-section cn/isages that amendment to Cok¬ 
ing Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act and Coal 
Mines (Nationalisation) Act shall be deemed to 
have come into froce on 1-5-72 and 1-5-73 respec¬ 
tively and the remaing pren Lions of the Amendment 
Act shall come into force on 7-10-1986. Thereafter 
the Amendment Act derides different section of 
that Act n& 'amendment’ by side notes and different 
sections as 'substitution’ by skk notes as well as by 
incorporating such ‘substitution' in the core of subs¬ 
tituted section. Thus, the scheme of the Amend¬ 
ment Act is clearly to recopnisc some sections of 
that Act as ‘amendment’ and some sections as ‘sub¬ 
stitution’. Section 17 of the Coking Coal Mines (Na¬ 
tionalisation) Act and Section 14 of the Coal Mines 
(Nationalisation) Act have been substituted by new 
sections. Such being the position, the intendment of 
the legislature, in mv view, was to substitute these 
two sections of Coking Coal Mints (Nationalisation) 


Act and Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act by 
new sections and thereby to give effect to the subs- 
titu:ed sections with effect from 7-10-86 aid not 
from 1-5-72 or l L 5-73. But the Hon’blc Patna 
High Court has decided that amendment includes 
substitution and so the substituted sections shall be 
deemed to have come into effect on 1-5-72 or 
1-5-73 as the came may be. Although there appears 
to have scope to differ with the Hon’ble Court on 
this point, this Tribunal is hound by the decision of 
Hon’ble Court. Hence, it shall be * concluded that 
the concerned workman cannot vindicate his right 
in the present lderencc case on the strength of pro¬ 
vision of Section 17 of the Coking Coal Mines (Na¬ 
tionalisation) Act, 1972. 

13. Shri J. D. Lai has drawnup my attention to 
Section 18(3) of the Industrial Disputes Act and 
submitted that the concerned workman can vindi¬ 
cate his right under the provision of this section, 
I am afraid. 1 cannot agree with him on this point 
because the application of Section 18(3) is depen¬ 
ded upon a settlement arrived at in the course of 
conciliation proceeding under the Act oi Arbitration 
Award or an Award of a Labour Court, Tribunal 
or National Tribunal which has become enforceable. 
Such settlement or Arbitration Award or Award 
shall be binding on —■ 

(a) all parties to the industrial dispute; 

(b) all other parties summoned to appear ill 
the proceeding as parlies to the dispute; 

(c) where a party referred to in clase (a) or 
clause (b) is an employee, his heir, suc¬ 
cessor or assigns in respect of the establish¬ 
ment to which the dispute relates; 

(d) where a party referred to in clause (a) or 
clause (b) is composed of workmen, all 
persons who were employed in the esta¬ 
blishment or part of the establishment, as 
the case may be, to which the dispute relates 
on the date of the dispute and all persons 
who subset]uentlv become employed in that 
establishment or part.” 

Tn the present case the pleadings of the parties 
do not disclose that any settlement wa* arrived at 
during the course of conciliation proceeding or that 
an Arbitration Award or an Award of Labour Court, 
Tribunal or National Tribnal which has become en¬ 
forceable was passed. Hence, I have no hesitation to 
overrule the content km of Shri J, D, Lai on this 
point, 

11. Accordingly, the following ay^lrd is rendered- 
tliat the present lcfcrencc case is not maintainable. 
Consequently, it is held that the action of the manage¬ 
ment of Amlabad Colliery of Bhowra Area No. 
XI of M,s. B.C.C. Ltd.. P.O. Bffowra, Dist. 
Dhanbad, in not taking Shri Bhagwat Dtisad, M| 
Loadci 4 back in employment is justified. 

Tn the circumstances of the case, I award no cost. 

This is my award. 

S. K. MJTkA, Piesiding Officer

AWARD 

1. The Central Government, Ministry of Labour, 
vide reference number L12012| 179|85-D.1I(A) dt 
18-6-86, has referred the following dispute for ad¬ 
judication to this Tribunal — 

Whether the action of the management of Allaha¬ 
bad Bank relating to their chowk Varanasi 
Branch, in terminating 'the services of Sri 
Paras Nath, S|o Sri Lalji w.e.f* 15-2-82 
and not considering him for further em¬ 
ployment while recruiting fresh hands is 
justified ? If nO. L , to what relief is the work¬ 
man concerned entitled ? 

Again the CenraJ Goverlnnren vide its notification 
number L-120J2)X78|85-D. 11(A) dt. nil has refer¬ 
red the following dispute for adjudication to this 
Tribunal — 

Whether the: action of the management of Allaha¬ 
bad Bank relating to their chowk Varanasi 
Branch, in terminating the services of Sri 
Amar Nath s|o Sri Lalji w.e.f. 15-2-82 
and not considering him for further em- 
plomcnt while recruiting fresh hands is justi¬ 
fied ? If not, to what relief is the workman 
concerned entitled ? 

2. These two cases were consolidated by my learned 
predecessor Sri R. B. Srivastava, vide his order dt, 
28-11-86 passed in I.D. No. 95[86, I.D. No. 95|S6 
was made the leading case* 

3. Boi.h Sii Paras Nath and Sri Amar Nath are 
brothers. Their main case is that they were appointed 
as peons cum farrash on 1-11-81, by the Branch 
Manager of Allahabad Bank Chowk, Varanasi. 
Their appoin + ment was neither for fixed period nor 
it was in any leave vacancy* However, their services 
were terminated iltegaly w.e.f. 15-2-82 (a.n.) 
without any rhyme or reason despite their satisfactory 
work without notice and in violation of the provision 
of section 25G LD. Act, They allege that in viola¬ 
tion of the provisions of section 25H I.D. Act, the 
General Manager, Divisional Office Allahabad Bank, 
Lucknow conducted the examination for the post of 
peon cum farrash on 14-10-84 and took interview 
on 27-2-85 without giving them any opportunity for 
the same. They also allege that being members of the 
Scheduled Os i :e Community they arc entitled to pre¬ 
ference in the master of appointment, Lastly, they 
allege that the management having not observed the 
provisions of secLon 25H of lh e I.D Act, the man¬ 
agement were guilty of UNPAIR LABOUR 
PRACTICE. 


4. The ease is contested by the management of 
Allahabad Bank. 1 hev plead- that the reference is bad 
in law. According to the management there would 
not have arisen the question of ttveir appointment on 
1-11-81 as 1-11-81 w.is Sunday. Then there is sim¬ 
ple denial of the facts alleged by the workman in their 
claim statements without specifically staling as to 
whether they had worked as peons cum farrash or 
not and if they had worked for how many days and 
during what period they had worked. There is also 
no specific denial of the fact whether the General 
Manager Divisional Office Lucknow had conducted 
any examination for the post of Peon-cum-Farrash on 
14-10-84 and had taken interview on 27-2-85 with¬ 
out calling the two workmen. From such a prestigious 
Nationalised Bank such written statements are not 
expected * 

5. In support of their case, both the workmen have 
examined themselves and have also relied upon the 
documentary evidence. On the other hand, the man¬ 
agement have examined Sri Gopal Das, an officer of 
the Bank posted at Kashividya Peeth Branch of the 
Bank at Varanasi. 

6. In his cross examination, Sri Paras Nath has 
deposed that he had worked toi 106 days which is 
not correct as will be evident from the copy of certi¬ 
ficate dated 16-2-82, copy Ext. W.l (I.D. No. 95|86) 
This certificate wa* issued by the Manager, Varanasi 
Branch. It shows that from November, 1981 to Feb¬ 
ruary, 1982, Ire had simply worked fof 89 days. The 
document has been admitted by the authorised re¬ 
presentative for the management. 

7. Almost same is the case of Sri Amar Nath with 
regard to the number oT his working days. Tn cross 
examination he has deposed that he had worked con¬ 
tinuously from 1-11-81 to 16-2-82. But this state¬ 
ment gets belied from the copy of certificate dated 
17-2-82 copy Ext. W-l (in I.D. No. 105186J filed by 
him. This certificate has also been admitted by the 
authorised representative for the management. The 
certificate was issued by the Manager Varanasi Branch. 
It shows that like his brother he too had worked for 
89 days only. 

8. The actual number of days from 1st November 
to 16th February, 1982 comes to 198 days. It follows, 
therefore, that both of them had not worked conti¬ 
nuously but had worked intermittently. 

9. Tn his cros s examination Sri Amar Nath has de¬ 
posed that his appointment was against a permanent 
vacancy. But this docs not seem to be correct. He 
has said that he was given an appointment letter but 
it was taken back from him when duty was given to 
him. However, he has stated that from the Employ¬ 
ment Exchange his name was sent for temporary ap¬ 
pointment. If it were so, his appointment could not 
have been against any permanent vacancy. The man¬ 
agement witness has clearly deposed in his cross exa¬ 
mination that both the workmen were kept in the 
leave vacancies. After them one Sri Ram Lai was 
kept in the leave vacancy. However, even Sri Ram 
Lai is no longer in the employment of the Bank. So 
is the case of his brother Sri Paras Nath. 

10. Having worked for 89 days only, in their cases, 
the provisions of section 25G read with Rule 77 of 




[qnT II —m 3 (ii) ] ^ 7 ™ : 


I.D. (Central) Rules, 1957, and section 25H I. D. 
Act read with Rule 78 of the 1. D. Central Rules, 
1957 would not apply. This was so held by me in 
I.D, No. 94 of 1986 Sri Mani Ram Versus Regional 
Manager Central Bank of India in which an award 
was given by me. In the instant case from the evi¬ 
dence of the v/ojkman it appears that they were 
given opportunity for fresh employment. In para 4 
of his statement in cross examination, Sri Amar Nath 
has deposed that after the termination of his services, 
he was given two chances by the Bank. He appear¬ 
ed in the written test both the times and both the 
times he appeared in the interview as well. Similarly 
Sri Paras Nath lias deposed that after the termina¬ 
tion of his services he appeared in the written test 
conducted by the bank twice and also appeared in 
the interview both the times. So it does not lie in 
their mouth to •my that they were not given any oppor¬ 
tunity for re-employment. As said by me above in 
their case, the question of giving of any opportunity 
of re-employment did not arise as the provisions of 
Section 25H read with Rule 78 of I.D, Central Rules 
195 7 do not fr*pply in their case. 

10. In para 5 of his statement in cross examination 
Sri Amar Nath has deposed that after the termination 
of his services, the bank employed some persons 
temporarily but subsequently their services were ter¬ 
minated by the bank. So the question of Unfair 
Labour Practice lo which (hey have referred in their 
claim statement does not arise. 

11 . T may stale here that for the application of 
Rule 77 & 78 it is necessary that a workman should 
have been in continuous service for not less than 
one year prior to the termination of his services. 

12. A salient fact has appeared in the deposition 
of the management witness. The management wit¬ 
ness has deposed that the workman Sri Amar Nath 
has got employment in Punjab National Bank and 
he is at present posted in the said Bank’s Bianch at 
Mirzapur. No suggestion to the contrary has been 
thrown to him in bis cross examination. 

13. Tn this case 22-4-92, was fixed for hearing of 
arguments. O 11 21-4-92, written arguments with 
extracts from rulings typed on paper purported to 
havr been sent bv the two workmen were received 
in the office of the Tribunal bv post. While arguing 
the ease on behalf of the workman, Sri K. N Soni, 
the authorised representative of these two workmen. 
Informed the Tribunal that Sri V. N. Sekhari had 
informed him on telephone about the written argu¬ 
ments having been sent by post to the Tribunal, Sri 
Soni expressed his ignoreiice about (he nature of 
written araim< nts. He submitted that his onlv argu¬ 
ment in the two cases would be on the point that (h^ 
management were guilty of unfair labour practice and 
no other point. 

14. Hie wiiiten arguments, there is no disnuffi 
about the fact, aie in the hand writing of Sri V N. 
Sekhari who in the beginning had been the authorised 
representative for these two workmen and who later- 
on on 9-7-90 withdraw his authoriiv, Aftei the 
withdrawal of his authority Sri M. 1 ak Advocate 
filed Ids authority on behalf of the two woikmen but 
he too withdraw Ms authority on 5-3-91. The verv 
ritme day Sri FT N, Soni filed his authority and since 


yrrf 4, i9i ja/mms u, 1914 -967 


then he has been appearing in the ease on behalf of 
these two workmen. In the written arguments the 
address of (hese two workmen is given as C|o Sri 
V, N. Sekhari, 26|104, Birhamt Road, Kanpur. 

15. i fail to understand how Sri V. N. Sekhari, had 
come into (lie picture after the withdrawal of his 
authority as back on 9-7-90. Neither of the two 
workmen appealed on 22-4-92, the date fixed for 
hearing arguments in the case. They also did not 
cancel the authority executed by him in favour of 
Sri K. N. Soni. As said by me above Sri Soni had 
argued the case on behalf of the woikmen and he 
never gave out that his authority had been cancelled 
by the (wo workmen. In the circumstances, it ap¬ 
pears that Sri Sekhari, had misutilised the signatures 
ot these two workmen whose signatures he might 
have obtained on the blank paper. As earlier 
remarked by me had the workmen been a voluntary 
party to the written arguments thev would have 
surely attended the court on 22-4-92 and given ins¬ 
tructions to their authorised representative Sri K. N. 
Soni to file the written arguments on their behalf, 
Hence no notice can be taken of the wrilten argu¬ 
ments which aie in the hand writing o ? Sri V. N. 
Sekhari. 

17. As said t hove by me Sri Soni appearing on 
behalf of the workmen simply pressed the point of 
unfair labour practice on the part of the management. 
He is a seasoned person who has been conducting rhe 
cases of the workmen. Had he found any substance 
in any other arguments he would have surely argued 
those points as well, 

18. Any way let us have a passing look on the 
rulings cited bv Sri V. N, Sekhari in his written argu¬ 
ments. Rulings referred to by him on section 250 
and 25 }-1 read with Rules 77 and 78 of I.D. (Central) 
Rules, 1957, arc those most of which have been dis¬ 
cussed in detail by me in my award given in Indus¬ 
trial Dispute No. 94 of 1986, Mani Ram Versus 
Centra! Bank of India, Kanpur. Some of the rulings 
are also on certain preposition of law, such as where 
the termination ot services is void ubinitio reinstate¬ 
ment with full back wages is the normal rule and that 
ordinal ily every termination of service is retrench¬ 
ment. There is no dispute about there preposition of 
law. Some more rulings have been cited but they 
have no hearing on the facts of rhe present case. 

19. Therefore, from the above discussion of facts 
and circumstances, it is held that (he action of the 
management of Allahabad Bank in terminating the 
service of the two workmen w.e,f. 15-2-82 is justi¬ 
fied. Consequently they are held entitled to no relict. 
Further held that they were not entitled to any 
opportunity being given for re-employ men t under 
section 2^H of the I.D. Act, although, as admitted 
by both the workmen, they had been given two oppor¬ 
tunities bv the management. So on this count as 
wffil as the action of the management is held as 
jus Li lied. 

20. Both the references are answered accordingly, 
ket a copy of this award be placed on (lie record of 
I.D. No. 105 86 .

27. It was submitted by the said Transferee Bank 
that automatic absorption as claimed by the said 
DRPs or all of them will not be justified, as many of

were in service about 10 years ago, and no dispute was

Bank and All India Punjab National Bank Employees 
Federation, regarding the appointment of temporary 
workman, who had worked in subordinate cadre, not

be looked into or brought into action in this preced¬ 
ing, as the said DRPs or all of them, because of their 
workings, were not entitled to be considered as em¬ 
ployees of subordinate cadie, when admittedly they 
were casual employees and there is no appropriate 
legal evidence to establish that they have worked for 
240 days in a year or more. To support such submis¬ 
sions, reference was made to the Bench decision of 
Madras High Cofirt in the case between State Bank 
of India, Madras and Central Government Industrial 
Tribunal and Anr., 1991(1) LLJ 155. That decision, 
has of course indicated that actual working lor 240 
days during a period of 12 months is sufficient to make 
continuous service for a period of one year and there 
is no necessity for subsistance of contract of employ¬ 
ment during entire period of 12 months. Further re¬ 
ference was made to the Supreme Court decision in 
the case of State of Tamilnadu & Ors. Vs-Nalpai 
Cotton Mills and Ors., 1991(1) LLJ 35, for the pur¬ 
pose of showing the necessary requirements, which 
are to be established for the purpose of establishing 
the nature of service, which will entitle the employees 
concerned, for appointment in the case of the present 
nature. In the above case the words “Continuous ser¬ 
vice”. as used in Section 3 of the Tamilnadu Industrial 
Establishment (Conferment of Permanent Status to 
Workmen) Act, 1981, was construed and it has been 
indicated that service period after discharge or non 
employment, though skich discharge may be illegal, 
cannot be counted for continuous service.

37. To consider the preliminary points as men¬ 
tioned in paragraph 26 above. T think, the said

[wr U — sr* a (ii)] £ /„ 1992/*,unf 1 3 , „ 1 e 14 


cnce. It was claimed by the said Sarnky tht even- 
through the said DPT\> were and arc entitled to 
automatic absorption, yet th'e said Transferee Bank 
is not fulfilling their obligations in that respect and 
in fact, al hough \u* i nciej to such posts were and 
are sti 1 ] available i ud die r ud DRPs are working 
since 1978, they are not being absorbed and instead 
thereof, those woil 1 are hu. <* don’e by appointing 
casual and temporary man. The fac", there was a 
Committee of Directors of the said Bank, A o look 
into the matter, in the liglr of the demands of the 
DRPs, then there was a Meritorium Ext. W-5 and 
ul imately there was the said Scheme Ext. W-7 evo¬ 
lved, cannot be doubted or disputed. The said 
Scheme F\t. \\ -7 has med the terms in Clause 
(10) “All cmil wees of the Transferor Bank shall 
continue in service and bt. deemed to have been 

appointed by the Tiuncferee Bank .” and 

thus*, we should try and find out, what is the mean¬ 
ing and effect of <mch “deeming cause" The word 
“deemed", as would appear in celebrated decisions, 
makes it clear that a thing is not what, it the eye 
of law, it is supposed to be or the thing is in fact 
what it is taken to be, no question of supposition 
would arise and a indicated in words and phrases 
judicially defined by Ralan Burraw". Generally 
speaking, when you talk of a thing being deemed to 
be something you do not mean to say that it is that 
which is to be deemed to be. It is rather an admis¬ 
sion that it is not what it is to be deemed to be, 
and that notwithstanding It Is not that .... “parti¬ 
cular thing, nevertheless.It is to be deemed to 

that thing". It has also been indicated theie tint 

“The word “deemed".is more commonly used 

for the purposes of creating . a statutory fie- 

tion ., that is, for the purpose of creaffng 

the meaning of same term to a subject matter which 
it docs not proparly designate. It should be noted 
that in the case of Mysore State Road Transport 
Corporation—'Vs-—Khaja Mohiuddin, AIR 1969 
Mysore 41, ft has been indicated that the word 
deeemed to have been appoated* would mean that 
they have not really so appointed. As observed in 
R.V, Nafolk Country- Council, (1891) 60 LJ.O.B. 
379, when a thing is- to be deemed something else, 
it is to be treated as that something else with the 
attendant consequences, but it is not that something 
else. It has 0 B 0 been observed in East End Dwelling 
Co. Ltd. Vs. Puisbury Borough Council, 1952 A.C. 
109 that if one is bidden to treat an imaginary state 
of affairs as real, que must also imagine as real the 
consequences and incidents which, if the imagine*'' 
state of affairs had in fact existed, must inevitably have 
followed from it.

AWARD 

The workman concerned (hereinafter referred to 
as the said workman), whose case was represented by 
Punjab National Bank Employees’ Union (herein¬ 
after referred t: as the said Union), claimed to have 
joined the services of the Punjab National Bank 
(hereinafter rtfctred to as the said Bank), as Clerk- 
cum-Cashier, at the Keshiary Branch, on September 
26, 1984. 

2. On or about June 5, 1985, by the notice Ext 
W-l, die Regional Manager of the said Bank, amongst 


6. It will also appear from Ext. M-2 that on Sep¬ 
tember 17, 1985, the said workman lias been selected 
for tite same post and in another Branch and that 
to on his application and it was argued now by his 
learned representative appearing before this Tribunal 
that on such, he duly joined in that post and now, 
he is at Calcutta 

7. Being dissatisfied, the following dispute - 

“Whether the action of the management of Pun¬ 
jab National Bank, 15, ' Park Street, 
Calcutta-700016 in not considering the ap¬ 
plication of Shri T. K. Roy for the past of 
Clerk■ cnm-Cashipr-in-eharge, Category *A’ 
and not posting him at Patharghata Branch, 
District 24 Parganas and thereby depriving 
him of the benefit of better allowances from 
the date he has been deuied of his legiti¬ 
mate claim is justified? If not, to what 
relief is the workman entitled ?”. 

was raised and referred for adjudication before this 
Tribunal under Section 10(1) (d) read with Section 
(2A1 of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, by the 
appropriate government vide Order No. L-19012',46! 
86-D.IV(A) dated 16-4-1987. 

8. The facts as indicated earlier were not very 
much in dispute and the said union claimed that the 
notices kt Ext. W-l and W-l'l were not received bv 
the concerned Branch in appropriate time and in .fact, 




[TOf II—^ 3 - (u)] 


*trct rf 4, 1992/3^^ rx i9u- 


■he did not v a?te any time and really, immediately on 
June 19, 1985, when the Branch in question received 
the notice, he applied on the same, day,, which again, 
was duly sent by the Branch and in fact, he had no 
hand in delayed receipt of his application. It would 
appear from Ext. M-3, which was received in evi¬ 
dence on dispensing the formal proof that the appli¬ 
cation in question did really reach the Regional Office, 
at a date late than the date, when the selection pro¬ 
cess was completed and that is the reason why the 
same could not be considered. Ckis evidence can¬ 
not be overlooked, although it cannot aho be disput¬ 
ed that this is really a very unfortunate case, where 
the said workman has suffered for no fault on his 
part. He duly, bon a fide and diligently acted in mak¬ 
ing his application, but the same, beyond the control 
of every body, reached the Regional Office at a stage 
later than the ‘election and it cannot be found an evi¬ 
dence that the said Bank had any laches. 

9. ft was also admitted that only three months after 
the selection, the said workman has got the selection 
in the same post in a different Branch and now he 
is here at Calcutta. 

10. In view of the above, the learned representa¬ 
tive of the said Union submitted that if at all, the said 
workman has suffered some financial loss, for about 
three months. Even if the case is like that, It cannot 
be held and observed on the evidence as adduced 'hat 
there was anv complicity of the said Bank, in depriv¬ 
ing die benefits to the said workman or such action 
was taken intentionally or with any ill motive. 

11. That being the position, I think, no case has 
been r* ode out to answer the Reference in the affir¬ 
mative and in fevour of the said workman. 

12. This is my Award. 

MANASH NATH ROY, Presiding Officer
